Global Convergence of Gradient EM for Over-Parameterized Gaussian Mixtures
Mo Zhou, Weihang Xu, Maryam Fazel, Simon S. Du

Abstract
Learning Gaussian Mixture Models (GMMs) is a fundamental problem in machine learning, with the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm and its popular variant gradient EM being arguably the most widely used algorithms in practice. In the exact-parameterized setting, where both the ground truth GMM and the learning model have the same number of components m m , a vast line of work has aimed to establish rigorous recovery guarantees for EM. However, global convergence has only been proven for the case of m = 2 m=2 , and EM is known to fail to recover the ground truth when m ≥ 3 m\geq 3 . In this paper, we consider the over-parameterized setting, where the learning model uses n > m n>m components to fit an m m -component ground truth GMM. In contrast to the exact-parameterized case, we provide a rigorous global convergence guarantee for gradient EM. Specifically, for any well separated GMMs in general position, we prove that with only mild over-parameterization n = Ω ⁡ ( m ​ log ⁡ m ) n=\Omega(m\log m) , randomly initialized gradient EM converges globally to the ground truth at a polynomial rate with polynomial samples. Our analysis proceeds in two stages and introduces a suite of novel tools for Gaussian Mixture analysis. We use Hermite polynomials to study the dynamics of gradient EM and employ tensor decomposition to characterize the geometric landscape of the likelihood loss. This is the first global convergence and recovery result for EM or Gradient EM beyond the special case of m = 2 m=2 .



1
Introduction

Gaussian Mixture Models (GMMs) are widely used across machine learning applications, where their ability to model complex, multimodal data distributions makes them a fundamental tool in probabilistic
modeling.
In this paper, we consider the canonical problem of learning Gaussian mixtures with isotropic covariances. Here the ground truth distribution, a
d
-dimensional
m
-component Gaussian mixture model (
m
-GMM), is given by the probability density function
p_{*}
:

p_{*}({\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\pi_{i}^{*}\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*};{\bm{x}}),

(1)

where
\pi_{i}^{*}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
are the mixing weights with
\sum_{i}\pi_{i}=1
,
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
are the ground truth mean vectors, and
\phi({\bm{\mu}};{\bm{x}})\coloneqq(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(-\left\|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{\mu}}\right\|_{2}^{2}/2)
is the p.d.f. of a Gaussian with mean
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
and covariance
{\bm{I}}_{d}
. The goal is to recover the true distribution
p_{*}
with observed data sampled from it.

The EM and gradient EM algorithms.

For learning hidden variable models such as GMMs, perhaps the most popular algorithm used in practice is the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm
[
14
]
. Using a model
n
-GMM defined as

p({\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{i};{\bm{x}}),

(2)

the EM algorithm iteratively updates the mixing weights
\bm{\pi}\coloneqq(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n})^{\top}
and model means
{\bm{\mu}}\coloneqq({\bm{\mu}}_{1}^{\top},\ldots,{\bm{\mu}}_{n}^{\top})^{\top}
to learn the ground truth distribution
p_{*}
. Note that the number of component Gaussians in the model (
n
) and the ground truth (
m
) can differ. In practice, a popular lightweight variant of EM, called gradient EM, is often used when vanilla EM update is too computationally expensive. In the
t^{\text{th}}
iteration, population EM and gradient EM updates consist of two steps:

•

Expectation (E) step: For
i=1,\ldots,n
, compute the membership weight function
\psi_{i}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}^{+}
, the posterior probability of
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
being sampled from the
i^{\text{th}}
model Gaussian:

\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})=\Pr({\bm{x}}|i)=\frac{\pi_{i}^{(t)}\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{(t)};{\bm{x}})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\pi_{k}^{(t)}\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{k}^{(t)};{\bm{x}})}.

•

Maximization (M) step: Define
Q
function

Q(\bm{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}|\bm{\pi}^{(t)},\bm{\mu}^{(t)})=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{*}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}({\bm{x}};\bm{\pi}^{(t)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)})\left(\log\pi_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right].

Standard EM updates
\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}
as the maximizer of
Q
function:
(\bm{\pi}^{(t+1)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t+1)})=\arg\max_{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}Q(\bm{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}|\bm{\pi}^{(t)},\bm{\mu}^{(t)})
. Gradient EM replaces this sometimes costly maximization step with one gradient step on
Q
. Let
\eta\in\mathbf{R}^{+}
be the learning rate. The update rule is:

(\bm{\pi}^{(t+1)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t+1)})\leftarrow(\bm{\pi}^{(t)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)})+\eta\nabla_{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}Q(\bm{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}|\bm{\pi}^{(t)},\bm{\mu}^{(t)}).

(3)

Here
\bm{\pi}^{(t)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)}
denotes the collection of training parameters in
t^{\text{th}}
iteration and
\pi_{i}^{(t)},{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{(t)}
are similarly defined.
To focus the optimization analysis, we primarily study the population update in large sample limit without statistical errors (see,
e.g.
,
[
43
,
42
]
). We later extend our results to the finite-sample setting with polynomial sample complexity.

Exact-parameterized GMM.
Establishing a rigorous recovery guarantee for (gradient) EM on GMM has been an important problem in machine learning theory, due to its popularity in practice. The most natural setting here is exact-parameterization, where the model uses the same number of component Gaussians as the ground truth (
n=m
). Since the seminal result
[
7
]
provided the first convergence guarantee for
2
-GMMs, a long line of work has followed
[
16
,
43
,
24
,
45
]
. However, proving global convergence for general
m
-GMMs with
m\geq 3
, even in the well-separated setting, turns out to be intractable due to the existence of bad local minima
[
22
]
.

Over-parameterized GMM.
Motivated by the impossibility result in the exact-parameterized setting, another series of papers study the behavior of (gradient) EM in over-parameterized setting where the model uses more component Gaussians than in the ground truth (
n>m
)
[
15
,
11
,
13
,
9
,
42
]
.
This line of work is motivated by the implicit belief that over-parameterization might improve the optimization landscape of GMMs, thus help (gradient) EM avoid spurious local minima.
The following important conjecture (see Section 5 of
[
9
]
) lies at the core of this line of research:

Conjecture
.

Overparametrization enables (gradient) EM to converge globally on Gaussian Mixtures.

In this paper, we prove the above conjecture by showing the global convergence of gradient EM for general over-parameterized GMMs. Our main result can be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1

(Main result, informal version of Theorem
3.1
)
.

Assume the ground truth Gaussian means
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are well separated, bounded and non-degenerate (Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
). Consider the problem of using over-parameterized GMM (
2
) to learn the ground truth GMM (
1
). Suppose we use randomly initialized population gradient EM with near-optimal weight updates (Algorithm
1
) with polynomially small step size
\eta
. If the amount of over-parameterization is at least logarithmically large, then with high probability, gradient EM converges globally to ground truth with rate
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{T^{2}}\right)
.

For the detailed assumptions and algorithmic setup we refer the readers to Section
2
.
We compare our result with prior art in Table
1
.

Main Contributions.

The major contributions of this paper can summarized as follows:

•

We prove that over-parameterized gradient EM converges globally on well-separated GMMs. This is the first global convergence result of EM or gradient EM for general
m
-GMMs. Comparisons of our result with previous analysis of EM/gradient EM are listed in Table
1
.

•

We introduce novel tools from other fields and apply them to the analysis of gradient EM:

–

We use tensor decomposition tools from
[
2
,
19
]
and the idea of test functions to characterize the geometric optimization landscape of GMM learning.

–

We use Hermite polynomials to analyze the training trajectory of gradient EM.

Notably, these tools were originally developed for other purposes, and a key contribution of our work is connecting them to the analysis of EM. Tensor decompositions and Hermite polynomials have been used for provable GMM learning
[
2
,
28
]
, test functions have been used in the analysis for shallow neural networks
[
47
]
and homotopy methods in PDEs
[
27
]
.

]

,

]

,

]

]

Table 1
:
Comparison of our result with prior art on (gradient) EM for GMMs. "Suboptimal" denotes the negative result showing high probability convergence to suboptimal local minima. For local convergence, initialization is close to the ground-truth. For global convergence and suboptimal convergence, the initialization is random.

1.1
Related works

EM and gradient EM algorithm.

The literature on theoretical analysis of (gradient) EM is long and vast
[
39
,
44
]
.
A flurry of works proved global convergence for exact-parameterized
2
-GMMs
[
7
,
16
,
43
,
40
,
38
]
. Another line of works focus on local convergence for general exact-parameterized
m
-GMMs
[
45
,
48
,
24
,
35
]
. As mentioned in Section
1
, a general global convergence analysis in exact-parameterized regime is intractable due to the spurious local minima
[
22
]
. In the over-parameterized regime,
[
11
,
13
,
12
,
42
]
studied global convergence of using multi-component GMMs to learn a single Gaussian (
m=1
). Providing evidence for benefits of over-parameterization,
[
15
]
proposed a variant of EM with pruning and
[
9
]
studied the geometric landscape of likelihood loss for GMMs. Yet none of the above works imply global convergence of (gradient) EM beyond the special case of
m=2
.

Learning Gaussian Mixtures.

Since
[
10
]
, algorithmic approaches other than EM for GMM learning have a long and rich history. A series of works develop theoretical algorithms with sum-of-squares proofs
[
20
,
26
,
28
]
, though they are not practical to implement.
Their theory can cluster GMMs with minimal separation
\Omega(\sqrt{\log m})
, matching the information theoretic lower bound
[
34
]
. Another line works studies tensor-decomposition methods
[
19
,
2
,
1
,
18
]
.
In practice, however, EM remains the most widely used method due to its efficiency and simplicity.
Our work aims at proving theoretical guarantees for gradient EM and is orthogonal to these algorithmic results. Nevertheless, our analysis connects the technical tools developed in these works (Hermite polynomials and orthogonal tensor decomposition) with the analysis of gradient EM.

2
Preliminaries

Notation.

Define
[n]\coloneqq\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. For any vector
{\bm{v}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, we use
\bar{{\bm{v}}}\coloneqq\frac{{\bm{v}}}{\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|}
to denote its normalization. Denote
\pi_{\min}^{*}\coloneqq\min_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}
as the minimum weight of ground truth Gaussian Mixtures. We denote the collection of model mixing weights and means with
\bm{\pi}\coloneqq(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n})^{\top}
and
{\bm{\mu}}\coloneqq({\bm{\mu}}_{1}^{\top},\ldots,{\bm{\mu}}_{n}^{\top})^{\top}
, respectively. We use the shorthand
\mathbf{E}_{x\sim j}[\cdot]\coloneqq\mathbf{E}_{x\in\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}}^{*}_{j},{\bm{I}})}[\cdot]
as the expectation over the
j^{th}
ground truth distribution. Denote
k
-th order tensor as
T\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
. For two tensors
T_{1},T_{2}\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
,
\left\langle T_{1},T_{2}\right\rangle
denotes the their Euclidean inner product.
We use
\mathcal{O},\Omega,\Theta
to hide constants and
a\lesssim b
to denote there exists an absolute constant
C
such that
a\leq Cb
.

KL divergence loss.

A well known property of gradient EM is its equivalence with gradient methods on the population likelihood. We first have the following definition of KL divergence loss.

Definition 1

(Loss function)
.

Define the loss function
\mathcal{L}
as the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence between the ground truth GMM and the model GMM as

\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})\coloneqq D_{\text{KL}}(p_{*}||p)=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{*}}[\ell(p({\bm{x}}))]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{*}}[\ell(p^{*}({\bm{x}}))],

(4)

where
\ell(z)=-\log(z)
is the negative log loss.

Since the minimum of
D_{\text{KL}}(p_{*}||p)
is achieved when
p=p_{*}
, global minimum of the loss
\mathcal{L}
is obtained if and only if the ground truth GMM is recovered. Then the following property connects gradient EM to gradient descent on
\mathcal{L}
.

Claim 2.1
.

For any
\bm{\pi}^{(t)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)}
, we have

\nabla_{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}Q(\bm{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}|\bm{\pi}^{(t)},\bm{\mu}^{(t)})=-\nabla_{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi}^{(t)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)}).

Claim
2.1
directly implies the equivalence between the gradient step on the
Q
function in gradient EM and one gradient step on the KL loss
\mathcal{L}
. Its proof is by direct algebraic calculation (see
[
22
,
42
]
). Therefore, gradient EM could be seen as gradient descent on the loss function
\mathcal{L}
. Our analysis crucially relies on this property and revolves around various properties of
\mathcal{L}
.

Algorithmic setup.

We consider a standard random initialization scheme
[
15
,
22
]
where the mean vectors
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{(0)}
are initialized as i.i.d. samples from the observed ground truth data. We also assume the mixing weights are initialized as equal for simplicity:
\pi_{i}=\frac{1}{n}
for all
i\in[n]
.

Careful readers might notice that the traditional definition of gradient EM update described in (
3
) is actually not well-defined for the mixing weights
\bm{\pi}
, as they reside in a
simplex
{\mathcal{P}}\coloneqq\{\bm{\pi}:0\leq\pi_{i}\leq 1,\forall i,\sum_{i}\pi_{i}=1\}
while gradient EM update is defined for unconstrained problems. Note that obtaining the maximum likelihood estimate w.r.t
\bm{\pi}
is a convex problem and can be efficiently solved by classic convex optimization approaches
[
6
,
21
,
33
]
. Therefore, we assume for simplicity that
\bm{\pi}
is updated in each iteration with a
\poly({\varepsilon})
-optimal solution of the convex subproblem
\min_{\bm{\pi}\in{\mathcal{P}}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)})
where
{\varepsilon}
is the target accuracy. Meanwhile
{\bm{\mu}}
is still updated with standard gradient EM. The final algorithm is described in Algorithm
1
.

Remark 1
.

Although Algorithm
1
considers near-optimal weight updates for simplicity, it can be seen as a limiting case of standard gradient EM with different step sizes for
\bm{\pi}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
. Specifically, setting step sizes
\eta_{\pi}
much larger than
\eta_{\mu}
(i.e., updating
\bm{\pi}
much faster than
{\bm{\mu}}
) leads to Algorithm
1
in the limit as
\eta_{\mu}/\eta_{\pi}\to 0
in each iteration. Similar algorithmic reductions are often used for the analysis of neural networks
[
4
,
31
,
37
,
3
,
5
]
.

Assumptions.

In this paper, we make the following standard assumptions on the ground truth GMM.

Assumption 1

(Non-degeneracy/General position)
.

There exists
\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\min}\in\mathbf{R}^{+}
such that
M_{2}^{*}=\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*\top}
is a rank
m
matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{\max}=\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{m}=\lambda_{\min}>0
.

Assumption 2

(Boundedness)
.

We assume the norms of ground truth Gaussian means are lower and upper bounded,
i.e.
,
\exists D_{\max},D_{\min}\in\mathbf{R}^{+}
such that
D_{\max}\geq\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|\geq D_{\min}\geq 4\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}\sqrt{dn},\forall i\in[m]
.

Assumption 3

(Well-separatedness)
.

The minimum separation between the ground truth Gaussians
\Delta\coloneqq\min_{i\neq j}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|
is lower bounded: there is a large enough constant
C
such that

\Delta\geq C\max\left\{\sqrt{\log(dnmD_{\max}/(\pi_{\min}^{*}\lambda_{\min}))},\sqrt{D_{\max}\sqrt{dn}},\sqrt{d/\pi_{\min}^{*}}\right\}.

Remark 2
.

The linear independence condition in Assumption
1
is often required by tensor decomposition methods
[
25
,
19
,
2
,
18
]
. The separation condition in Assumption
3
, while stronger than what is strictly necessary
[
34
]
, allows us to focus on the well-separated regime and simplifies the analysis.
Assumption
2
is just standard regularity condition and without loss of generality: since the GMM learning problem is translation invariant, one can always choose the origin point to ensure
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|
are lower bounded. (See Lemma
B.4
for details.)

All the above assumptions hold when
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},\Delta^{2}{\bm{I}}_{d})
for sufficiently large
\Delta
, in the high-dimensional regime where
m\ll d
. For simplicity, casual readers may assume
D_{\max},D_{\min},\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\min}=\Theta(\Delta)\gg\sqrt{d}
,
\max_{i}\pi_{i}^{*}/\min_{i}\pi_{i}^{*}=\Theta(1)
, and
n=\Theta(m\log m)\ll d
.

Algorithm 1

Population Gradient-EM with near-optimal weight updates

1:

Input:
Stepsize
\eta
,
iterations
T
, target accuracy
{\varepsilon}

2:

(Random) Initialization:

\forall i\in[n],{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{(0)}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}p^{*},\pi_{i}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{n}
.

3:

for

t=0
to
T

do

4:

\bm{\pi}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\text{$\poly({\varepsilon})$-optimal\ solution of convex subproblem}\arg\min_{\bm{\pi}\in{\mathcal{P}}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)})

5:

{\bm{\mu}}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)}+\eta\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}}Q(\bm{\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}}}|\bm{\pi}^{(t+1)},\bm{\mu}^{(t)})={\bm{\mu}}^{(t)}-\eta\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi}^{(t+1)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(t)})

6:

end

for

7:

Output:

{\bm{\mu}}^{(T)},\bm{\pi}^{(T)}

Hermite tensors.

The (one dimensional) probabilist’s Hermite polynomials are orthogonal polynomials w.r.t. the Gaussian measure defined as

\He_{k}(x)\coloneqq(-1)^{k}\exp\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}x^{k}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right).

For
d
-dimensional Gaussian, the degree-
k
Hermite tensor
\He_{k}({\bm{x}})\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
is a
k
-tensor. See Appendix
A.3
for a more detailed introduction. Hermite polynomials are a powerful tool for analysis of Gaussian Mixtures.
Our analysis relies on the following well-known property of Hermite tensors, which is also a key ingredient in many method-of-moments works for provable GMM learning
[
28
,
20
]
.

Claim 2.2
.

For any
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d},k\in\mathbf{N}
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}},{\bm{I}})}[\He_{k}({\bm{x}})]={\bm{\mu}}^{\otimes k}.

3
Main result

We now present our main result. It shows that with only logarithmic overparametrization, gradient EM can recover ground-truth model in polynomial time.

Theorem 3.1

(Main result)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, if number of components
n\gtrsim\frac{1}{\pi_{\min}^{*}}\log m
is large enough and stepsize
\eta\lesssim{\varepsilon}/\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})
is small enough, then with probability at least
1-1/\poly(m)
we have loss
\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}}^{(t)},\bm{\pi}^{(t)})\leq{\varepsilon}
after running Algorithm
1
for time
T=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{dn}/(\eta{\varepsilon}_{0})\right)+\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})/(\eta\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
, where
{\varepsilon}_{0}
is defined in Theorem
4.2
.

Moreover, when
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, for every
i\in[n]
either there exists ground-truth
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
such that
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}=0
, or
\pi_{i}=0
. That is, we recover the ground-truth model
p_{*}
.

Remark 3
.

While here we assume the separation
\Delta\geq\Omega(\sqrt{d})
(Assumption
3
), a simple trick of effective dimension can reduce this to
\Omega(\sqrt{m})
. (See Section 7.3.1 of
[
9
]
for details).

When
\frac{\max_{i}\pi_{i}^{*}}{\min_{i}\pi_{i}^{*}}=\Theta(1)
, the above result implies that we only need
n=\Theta(m\log m)
components to recover ground-truth
p_{*}
. This is the first global convergence and recovery result for EM or gradient EM beyond the special case of
m=2
. We believe both the convergence rate and the separation requirement in our assumptions can be improved, and we leave these refinements for future work.

Some previous works
[
15
]
consider variants of the EM algorithm utilizing a pruning process to remove redundant component Gaussians. Our proof in fact shows that gradient EM automatically prunes the redundant centers
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
as their weights
\pi_{i}
converge to
0
.

The proof is a 2-stage analysis that first shows loss reaches below certain threshold
{\varepsilon}_{0}
, and then to arbitrary small error
{\varepsilon}
. We introduce technical tools such as test function, Hermite polynomials to deal with the challenges, see more details in Section
4
.

In contrast to our result, it is known that with exact-parametrized GMM, gradient EM does not converge to global minima, even for well-separated GMMs satisfying Assumption
3
.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
22
]
, rephrase)
.

Consider the case
m=n
and
\pi_{i}=\pi_{i}^{*}=\tfrac{1}{m}
, and using first-order EM algorithm with stepsize
\eta<1
under random initialization. There exists a universal constant
c
, for any
m\geq 3
and any constant
C_{\rm{gap}}>0
, such that there is a well-separated
m
-
\mathrm{GMM}({\bm{\mu}}^{*})
with

\mathbb{P}\left(\forall t\geq 0,\;\mathcal{L}(\mu^{(t)})\leq\mathcal{L}(\mu^{*})-C_{\rm{gap}}\right)\geq 1-e^{-cm}.

The main idea behind Theorem
3.2
from
[
22
]
is that a randomly initialized exact-parameterized GMM will mismatch model means
{\bm{\mu}}
with the ground truth with high probability. In contrast, the coupon collector’s problem implies that a randomly initialized over-parameterized GMM will, with high probability, assign at least one
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
to each ground truth Gaussian, which is key to ensuring global convergence. Theorem
3.1
and
3.2
together demonstrate the key role over-parameterization plays in global convergence of gradient EM.

Sample complexity.

Our results naturally extend to the setting where only a polynomial number of samples is available. Instead of using the population loss and gradient in Algorithm
1
, we consider an online version of the algorithm that uses

N
fresh samples at each iteration, which is presented in Algorithm
2
in Appendix
H
.

Theorem 3.3

(Sample complexity)
.

Under the same setting as Theorem
3.1
and consider Algorithm
2
with
N=O(\max\{\frac{\sqrt{dn^{3}}(D_{\max}^{4}+d^{4}n^{4})}{\eta{\varepsilon}_{0}^{5}\delta},\frac{\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})}{\eta{\varepsilon}^{2.5}\delta}\})
fresh sample at every iteration, then with probability at least
1-1/\poly(m)-\delta
we have the same results as Theorem
3.1
.

This result shows that overparametrized gradient EM can efficiently learn GMMs using only polynomially many samples and in polynomial time, under our assumptions. The total sample complexity is
NT
, which scales as
{\varepsilon}^{-5}
(ignoring other dependencies) to achieve population loss
\mathcal{L}\leq{\varepsilon}
. Our goal is to establish polynomial bounds without aiming for tightness, and we leave the refinement of these estimates to future work.

:

n=5,10
, and
15
Gaussians to learn a
5
-GMM.

\mathcal{L}
, exact-parameterization v.s. over-parameterization;
Middle
: Exact-parameterized gradient EM converges to spurious local minima;
Right
: Global convergence trajectory of over-parameterized gradient EM.

Experiments.

Empirical simulations validating our result are provided in Figure
1
. We run randomly initialized gradient EM on a well separated ground truth
5
-GMM with model size
n=5,10,15
.
The left panel shows that over-parameterized gradient EM converges to the ground truth while exact-parameterized gradient EM does not.
The middle and right panels plot the training trajectory in exact v.s. over parameterized regimes. In the exact-parameterized case (middle panel) gradient EM gets trapped in a spurious local minima where
{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
converges to a local minimizer between two ground truth Gaussians. This corresponds to the "one-fits-many" local minima structure
[
9
]
. In the over-parameterized case (right panel) each ground truth is fitted with at least one
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
. In both regimes there are some
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
not converge to any of the ground truths. Instead, their mixing weights converge to
0
. This phenomenon corresponds to the dynamic weight challenge in our theoretical analysis (see
Challenge 1
).

4
Proof sketch: 2-stage analysis

We provide a sketch of the proof, which follows a two-stage analysis: global convergence (loss drops below a threshold
{\varepsilon}_{0}
, Sections
4.2
) and local convergence (loss reaches the target error
{\varepsilon}
, Section
4.3
).

We begin by introducing some notations. Let the components
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
be partitioned as
[n]=\cup_{\ell\in[m]}S_{\ell}
, where
S_{\ell}:=\left\{i\in[n]\;\middle|\;\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|_{2}\;\;\forall j\neq\ell\right\}

is the set of components closest to
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
among all ground truths. Define the potential function
U({\bm{\mu}})=\sum_{\ell\in[m]}\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2},

which measures the total distance of components from their nearest ground truths. We assume each
S_{\ell}
is non-empty when discussing
U
. We will show that
U
remains bounded during training. Also, let
\hat{\pi}_{\ell}:=\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}
denote the total weight in
S_{\ell}
.

4.1
Key challenges in analysis

Our analysis faces two main challenges due to overparametrization and multiple true components:

Challenge 1
: Dynamic weights.

Unlike prior works that often keep weights
\bm{\pi}
fixed, we update both weights
\pi_{i}
and means
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
. In the overparametrized setting (
n>m
), some weights
\pi_{i}
may converge to zero, preventing
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
to align with ground-truth components. This means identifiability cannot be guaranteed per component, only collectively over component groups.

Challenge 2
: Cross terms.

In the multi-cluster setting, a key challenge is controlling cross-term interactions between different ground-truth components. These terms persist even at the global optimum, typically at scale
\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
, making it hard to prove convergence to arbitrarily small error
{\varepsilon}
.
Existing results only guarantee error up to this scale, even for global minima
[
9
]
.

To overcome these challenges, we utilize Hermite polynomials, which are orthogonal under the Gaussian measure and naturally support order-wise decomposition. They are central to our identifiability argument that connects to tensor decomposition and test function framework developed in Section
5
.

4.2
Global convergence analysis

The goal of this part is to show that under random initialization, the loss will go below a certain threshold
{\varepsilon}_{0}=e^{-\Theta(\Delta^{2})}
in polynomial time.

We begin with random initialization. With only logarithmic overparametrization, we have that, with high probability, each ground-truth
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
has at least one
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
closer to it than to any other ground-truth center.

Lemma 4.1

(Initialization, informal)
.

Under random initialization
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}p_{*}
and
\pi_{i}=\frac{1}{n}
, if the number of components satisfies
n\gtrsim\frac{1}{\pi_{\min}^{*}}\log m
, then with high probability (at least
1-1/\poly(m)
):

1.

Each cluster
\ell
contains at least one component
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
such that
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}\in S_{\ell}
and
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{d}
.

2.

The initial potential
U
satisfies
B^{2}:=U({\bm{\mu}}^{(0)})\lesssim dn
and initial loss
\mathcal{L}^{(0)}\lesssim d
.

We have the main result for global phase below. All conditions are satisfied by random initialization. The full proof is deferred to Appendix
E
.

Theorem 4.2

(Main result for global phase)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, given any initialization
{\bm{\mu}}^{(0)},\bm{\pi}^{(0)}
such that loss
\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}}^{(0)},\bm{\pi}^{(0)})\lesssim d
,
U({\bm{\mu}}^{(0)})=B^{2}

and stepsize

\eta\lesssim{\varepsilon}/\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})
is small enough, then there exists threshold
{\varepsilon}_{0}=\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
such that loss
\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\pi}^{(T_{1})})\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}
within time
T_{1}=\mathcal{O}\left(B/(\eta{\varepsilon}_{0})\right).

We can see loss drops below
{\varepsilon}_{0}
within polynomial time.
The proof idea is to show the gradient norm has a lower bound, so that the algorithm can decrease the loss at every step, as shown below.

Theorem 4.3

(Informal)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, if
U({\bm{\mu}})\leq B^{2}
, then there exists a threshold
{\varepsilon}_{0}=\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
such that when
\mathcal{L}\geq{\varepsilon}_{0}
(i.e.,
t\leq T_{1}
) we have

\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\mu}}\mathcal{L}\right\|_{F}\gtrsim\frac{1}{B}\min\left\{\tfrac{1}{d},\mathcal{L}^{2}\right\}.

To prove this lower bound, we construct a descent direction and show it has non-trivial correlation with the gradient. The descent direction is intuitive: we move each
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}\in S_{\ell}
toward its nearest ground-truth mean
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
.

Lemma 4.4

(Informal)
.

For
t\leq T_{1}
, we have

\sum_{\ell}\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\langle{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*},\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}\mathcal{L}\rangle\gtrsim\min\left\{\tfrac{1}{d},\mathcal{L}^{2}\right\}.

To simplify the above gradient projection term, note that Assumption
3
and
U({\bm{\mu}})\leq B^{2}
imply that for
i\in S_{\ell}
,
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}\leq B\ll\Delta\leq\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|_{2}
. Intuitively, this allows us to bound the cross-group interaction (
Challenge 2
) since Gaussian density decays exponentially fast:

Lemma 4.5

(Informal)
.

For
i\in S_{\ell}
and
j\neq\ell
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\right]\lesssim\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2})).

Therefore, after ignoring all cross terms and letting
\bm{\widetilde{\psi}}_{\ell}({\bm{x}}):=\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*})
we get:

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in[m]}\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\langle{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*},\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}\mathcal{L}\rangle\geq\sum_{\ell\in[m]}\pi_{\ell}^{*}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\ell}\left[\left\|\bm{\widetilde{\psi}}_{\ell}({\bm{x}})\right\|^{2}\right]-\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2})).

Similar forms appear in prior work
[
42
]
under the single-component setting (
m=1
). Their approach introduces a dependency on
\pi_{\min}:=\min_{i}\pi_{i}
, which is problematic in our case since
\pi_{\min}
may be zero due to dynamic updates of
\bm{\pi}
(see
Challenge 1
). To address this, we develop a new approach using Hermite polynomials (see Appendix
A.3
for an introduction) and prove the following:

Lemma 4.6

(Informal)
.

For any
\ell\in[m]
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\ell}\left[\left\|\bm{\widetilde{\psi}}_{\ell}({\bm{x}})\right\|^{2}\right]\geq\left\|\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{\ell}^{*}}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{d}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{\ell}^{*}}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}.

The idea is to expand each
\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})
using Hermite polynomials:

\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\langle\bm{\hat{\psi}}_{i,k},\He_{k}({\bm{x}})\rangle,

with

\bm{\hat{\psi}}_{i,k}=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\ell}[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\He_{k}({\bm{x}})].
Note
\He_{0}({\bm{x}})=1
,
\He_{1}({\bm{x}})={\bm{x}}
so

\bm{\hat{\psi}}_{i,0}\approx\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{\ell}^{*}},\bm{\hat{\psi}}_{i,1}\approx\frac{\pi_{i}}{\pi_{\ell}^{*}}({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}).

Keeping only the first two Hermite terms and using the approximations yields the lower bound.

From above, we already have a gradient lower bound.
In Lemma
E.6
, we further relate these terms to the loss
\mathcal{L}
to obtain the desired gradient lower bound.

4.3
Local convergence analysis

In previous section, we show the loss can go below
{\varepsilon}_{0}=\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
. Here, we show that once below this threshold, the loss will further converge to any
{\varepsilon}>0
within time
\poly(d,m,n,1/{\varepsilon})
. The full proof is deferred to Appendix
C
.

Theorem 4.7

(Main result for local phase)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, given any
{\bm{\mu}}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\pi}^{(T_{1})}
such that loss
\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\pi}^{(T_{1})})\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}
with any
{\varepsilon}_{0}=\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
,
U({\bm{\mu}}^{(T_{1})})\leq B^{2}
and stepsize
\eta\lesssim{\varepsilon}/\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})
is small enough, then we have for any
t\geq T_{1}
:

\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}}^{(t)},\bm{\pi}^{(t)})\leq\frac{A^{2}}{(\eta(t-T_{1}))^{2}+\frac{A^{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{0}}},

where
A=\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})
.

The proof idea is same as in the global part to lower bound the gradient norm.

Theorem 4.8

(Informal)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, if loss
\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}},\bm{\pi})\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}
and
U({\bm{\mu}})\leq B^{2}
, then we have

\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\mu}}\mathcal{L}\right\|_{F}\gtrsim\frac{1}{\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})}\mathcal{L}^{3/4}.

Similar to the global part, we construct a descent direction by moving all nearby components
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
toward their closest ground-truth center
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
. We only focus on near components in local phase because it is possible that some
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
remain far from all
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
and weight
\pi_{i}
go to 0.

Lemma 4.9

(Descent Direction, Informal)
.

Denote
\widetilde{\pi}_{\ell}:=\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}(\delta_{\rm close})}\pi_{i}
is the total weights in
S_{\ell}(\delta_{\rm close})
that is
\delta_{\rm close}
-close to
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
(in
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
). Then

\sum_{\ell}\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}(\delta_{\rm close})}\frac{\pi_{\ell}^{*}}{\widetilde{\pi}_{\ell}}\langle\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}\mathcal{L},{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\rangle\geq\mathcal{L}/4.

To handle the gradient projection above, unlike Lemma
4.5
in the global part, the local analysis requires more care. We can now only tolerate perturbations proportional to the loss
\mathcal{L}
, rather than
\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
, since
\mathcal{L}
may be much smaller (see
Challenge 2
). Moreover, cross terms do not vanish even at the global optimum, as
\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}[\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*};{\bm{x}})/p_{*}({\bm{x}})]=\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
for
j\neq\ell
.

To address this, we control the cross terms via a Taylor expansion below. The first-order term gives the average component error
\pi_{\ell}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}-\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
, which dominates in the exact-parametrized setting but may vanish even when the loss is non-zero in the overparametrized case due to cancellation between components. The second-order term,
\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|^{2}
, measures the average squared distance to the ground truth and can become the dominant term in the overparametrized regime.

This shows why overparametrization complicates optimization analysis and may slow down convergence speed: the dominant signal may come from higher-order terms. Similar observations have been made in the analysis of shallow neural networks
[
47
,
46
,
41
]
and 1-GMM
[
42
]
.

Lemma 4.10

(Informal)
.

Recall
\hat{\pi}_{\ell}=\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}
is the total weights for group
S_{\ell}
. For cross term
j\neq\ell

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\frac{\pi_{i}\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{i};{\bm{x}})}{p({\bm{x}})}\right]\approx\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\ell}\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*};{\bm{x}})}{p({\bm{x}})}\right]+\left(\left\|\pi_{\ell}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}-\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}\right\|_{2}+\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)e^{-\Theta(\Delta^{2})}.

Now we can see that the error is proportional to not only
\exp(-\Theta(\Delta^{2}))
, but also average error
\pi_{\ell}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}-\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
and
\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, and weight error
\hat{\pi}_{\ell}-\pi_{\ell}^{*}
.
Intuitively, these terms should be small as loss is small. This can be viewed as an identifiability result since when loss is 0, we either recover
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}
or weights
\pi_{i}
goes to 0. We show it more precisely below (use
O_{*}(1)
to hide all
\poly(d,m,n,1/\pi_{\min}^{*},1/\lambda_{\min},D_{\max})
).

Theorem 4.11

(Informal)
.

When loss
{\varepsilon}:=L(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}
, then for any
\ell\in[m]
,

1.

weighted distance is small:
\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq O_{*}(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
.

2.

group weight is close:
|\pi_{\ell}^{*}-\hat{\pi}_{\ell}|\leq O_{*}(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
.

3.

average component is close:
\left\|\pi_{\ell}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}-\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq O_{*}(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
.

4.

close-by weight is large: There exists
\delta_{\rm close}=O_{*}({\varepsilon}^{1/4})
such that
\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}(\delta_{\rm close})}\pi_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2}\pi_{\ell}^{*}
.

While this result seems natural to be expected, recall
Challenge 1
that in the overparametrized regime (
n>m
) there is no classical one-to-one mapping as in the typical exact-parametrized regime (
m=n
). In particular, some
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}
may never converge to
{\bm{\mu}}_{\ell}^{*}
, but instead
\pi_{i}\to 0
. Thus, it is challenging to show such identifiability result in the overparametrized regime.

The
\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
dependency in these bounds are important for our analysis when we do the analysis to control the cross term (See Appendix
C
).
We prove this result by drawing a connection to other works that use tensor decomposition to learn GMMs
[
2
,
19
]
, which may be of independent interest. We discuss more details in Section
5
.

5
Identifiability in overparametrized regime

In this section, we outline the main ideas behind the proof of the identifiability result (Theorem
4.11
), which plays a key role in local convergence proof.
Proving identifiability of GMMs requires lower bounding the loss in terms of parameter error. At a high level, we use the concept of
test functions
to connect the KL loss to orthogonal tensor decomposition, and then leverage its identifiability.

While tensor decomposition and the method of moments have been widely used to provably learn GMMs
[
2
,
19
,
28
,
23
,
29
]
, our key contribution is connecting EM (minimizing KL divergence) to a corresponding tensor problem, which is new to our knowledge.

Test function.

Test functions allow us to extract information about a function
f
by pairing it with a carefully chosen
g
and analyzing their inner product
\langle f,g\rangle
. This idea appears in various fields (e.g., weak solutions in PDEs, hypothesis testing
[
17
]
, neural network analysis
[
47
,
46
]
).

In our setting, where the loss
\mathcal{L}
is small, we aim to construct a test function
g
such that:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}\left\|g\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{a})}}{{\geq}}\int_{\bm{x}}(p({\bm{x}})-p_{*}({\bm{x}}))g({\bm{x}}){\textnormal{d}}{\bm{x}}=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{*}}[g({\bm{x}})]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{b})}}{{\approx}}\text{Target term/property/...}

for a suitable norm
\left\|g\right\|
and target terms, for example, as appeared in Theorem
4.11
.

(a) Challenge in KL divergence.

The main difficulty in step (a) lies in handling the KL divergence. Prior work
[
47
,
46
]
typically focuses on the squared loss. However, the KL divergence is harder to handle analytically. A naïve Taylor expansion at
p_{*}
yields:
D_{\text{KL}}(p_{*}||p)\approx\frac{1}{2}\chi^{2}(p||p_{*})

but only gives an upper bound, not the lower bound we need.
We instead expand around a convex combination
p_{\theta}=\theta p+(1-\theta)p_{*}
for small
\theta\in[0,1]
, inspired by the homotopy analysis method
[
27
]
. Using results from
[
32
]
, we obtain (see more details in Appendix
B.1
):

Lemma 5.1

(Informal)
.

For
\theta\in(0,1)
we have

\mathcal{L}\geq(1-\theta)\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\theta}\right)\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{\theta}}\left[\left(\frac{p({\bm{x}})-p_{*}({\bm{x}})}{p_{\theta}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{2}\right].

We can now conclude step (a) by observing that (ignoring
\theta
dependecy):

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{x}}(p({\bm{x}})-p_{*}({\bm{x}}))g({\bm{x}}){\textnormal{d}}{\bm{x}}=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{\theta}}\left[\frac{p({\bm{x}})-p_{*}({\bm{x}})}{p_{\theta}({\bm{x}})}g({\bm{x}})\right]\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{\theta}}\left[g^{2}({\bm{x}})\right]}.

This enables the use of test function in the next step and we believe similar idea may generalize to other loss functions beyond KL loss and square loss.

(b) Hermite polynomials as test function leads to tensor decomposition.

To construct effective test functions, we use Hermite polynomials, which naturally align with Gaussian data and have been widely used in moment-based GMM learning algorithms
[
28
,
23
,
29
]
.

We define the test function as
g_{k}({\bm{x}})=\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\rangle
for any unit vector
{\bm{v}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
k=\{2,3,4\}
as the order of Hermite tensor used. From Claim
2.2
that
\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}},{\bm{I}})}[\He_{k}({\bm{x}})]={\bm{\mu}}^{\otimes k}
so

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{x}}(p({\bm{x}})-p_{*}({\bm{x}}))g({\bm{x}}){\textnormal{d}}{\bm{x}}=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{*}}[g({\bm{x}})]=\left\langle\sum_{i}\pi_{i}^{*}({\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*})^{\otimes k}-\sum_{i}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle.

Maximizing over all unit vectors on RHS gives

\left\|\sum_{i}\pi_{i}^{*}({\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*})^{\otimes k}-\sum_{i}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{\otimes k}\right\|_{2}.

Combining step (a)(b), we have for any
k\geq 0
(ignoring dependence on
\theta
from step (a) for clarity):

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}\left\|g_{k}\right\|\gtrsim\int_{\bm{x}}(p({\bm{x}})-p_{*}({\bm{x}}))g_{k}({\bm{x}}){\textnormal{d}}{\bm{x}}\geq\left\|\sum_{i}\pi_{i}^{*}({\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*})^{\otimes k}-\sum_{i}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{\otimes k}\right\|_{2}.

(c) From tensor decomposition to identifiability.

We now reduce this tensor problem to an orthogonal decomposition using whitening matrix as
[
2
]
. Under Assumption
1
, the second-moment tensor
{\bm{M}}_{2}^{*}
has full rank in the span of
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\}
with bounded eigenvalues in
[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
, allowing orthonormal decomposition:

{\bm{M}}_{2}^{*}={\bm{V}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{\bm{\Sigma}}&{\bm{0}}\\[1.29167pt]
{\bm{0}}&{\bm{0}}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr){\bm{V}}^{\top}
.
Let whitening matrix

{\bm{W}}\coloneqq{\bm{V}}\Bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}&{\bm{0}}\\[1.29167pt]
{\bm{0}}&\lambda_{\min}^{-1/2}{\bm{I}}_{d-m}\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr),

which ensures the whitening condition

{\bm{W}}^{\top}{\bm{M}}_{2}^{*}{\bm{W}}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{\bm{I}}_{m}&{\bm{0}}\\
{\bm{0}}&{\bm{0}}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.
Define whitened means
{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{*}\coloneqq\sqrt{\pi_{i}^{*}}{\bm{W}}^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}
and
{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{j}\coloneqq{\bm{W}}^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{j}
.
One can show
\{{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
are orthonormal as

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{\bm{I}}_{m}&{\bm{0}}\\
{\bm{0}}&{\bm{0}}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)={\bm{W}}^{\top}{\bm{M}}_{2}^{*}{\bm{W}}=\sum_{i}{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{\top}.

Applying
{\bm{W}}
to higher-order tensors yields:

Lemma 5.2

(Informal)
.

\left\|\sum_{l}{\pi_{l}^{*}}^{1-\tfrac{k}{2}}{{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{l}}^{*^{\otimes k}}-\sum_{i}\pi_{i}{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{\otimes k}\right\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda_{\min}^{-k/2}D_{\max}^{k}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}.

This gives an orthogonal tensor decomposition problem. When
\mathcal{L}=0
, the solution exactly recovers the true components up to permutation. For small
\mathcal{L}>0
, identifiability follows via perturbation analysis. Importantly, this method requires only low-order moments (e.g.,
k=2,3,4
). In overparametrized cases, we can still guarantee average-case recovery but not individual bound (Theorem
4.11
), as some mixture weights may vanish (see
Challenge 1
).

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we present the first global convergence result for gradient EM applied to general overparametrized Gaussian mixtures. Our analysis introduces new techniques, such as the use of Hermite polynomials and test functions, for studying gradient EM, and highlights its connection to method-of-moments approaches for provably learning GMMs. We believe this work represent an important first step toward a deeper understanding of the EM algorithm to broader settings. Moreover, we expect that our techniques can be extended to other related settings, including learning latent variable models and single- or multi-index models. Our analysis is limited in the sense that it focuses on well-separated GMMs. Extending the result to settings with weaker separation conditions, improved time and sample complexity, or the classical EM algorithm itself are interesting future directions.

Appendix A
Additional background and preliminaries

In this section, we present several useful facts. We give gradient related facts in Appendix
A.1
, tensor preliminaries in Appendix
A.2
, Hermite polynomials preliminaries in Appendix
A.3
, and some comments on Algorithm
1
in Appendix
A.4
.

A.1
Gradient related facts

We start with the classic Stein’s lemma.

Lemma A.1

(
[
36
]
)
.

For
{\bm{x}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{\mu}},\sigma^{2}{\bm{I}}_{d})
and differentiable function
g:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}
we have

\mathbf{E}_{\bm{x}}[g({\bm{x}})({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\mu}})]=\sigma^{2}\mathbf{E}_{\bm{x}}[\nabla_{\bm{x}}g({\bm{x}})],

if the two expectations in the above identity exist.

We use Stein’s lemma to derive an alternative form of the gradient, similar to the approach in
[
42
]
for the 1-GMM case. This new formulation is better suited for our analysis.

Lemma A.2
.

The gradient
\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}},\bm{\pi})
can be written as

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}},\bm{\pi})=\sum_{j\in[m]}\pi_{j}^{*}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{x}})\right]=\sum_{j\in[m]}\pi_{j}^{*}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\sum_{k\in[n]}\psi_{k}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{k}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*})\right].

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}},\bm{\pi})=\sum_{j\in[m]}\pi_{j}^{*}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{x}})\right].

We use Stein’s identity (Lemma
A.1
) to transform each summation term in RHS as

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{x\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*})\right]+\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*})\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\right]+({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*})\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\right].

Next we calculate

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\psi_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{x}})-\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\sum_{k\in[n]}\psi_{k}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{k}-{\bm{x}})=\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\left({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-\sum_{k\in[n]}\psi_{k}({\bm{x}}){\bm{\mu}}_{k}\right),

so

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\left(\sum_{k\in[n]}\psi_{k}({\bm{x}}){\bm{\mu}}_{k}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}+({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\sum_{k\in[n]}\psi_{k}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{k}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*})\right].

The gradient could thus be rewritten as

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{\mu}},\bm{\pi})=\sum_{j\in[m]}\pi_{j}^{*}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{x}})\right]=\sum_{j\in[m]}\pi_{j}^{*}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim j}\left[\psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\sum_{k\in[n]}\psi_{k}({\bm{x}})({\bm{\mu}}_{k}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*})\right].

∎

A.2
Tensor preliminaries

In this part, we give some basic notations and properties of tensors that we will use in the analysis.

For any tensor
T\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
, we index by multi-index
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\in[d]^{k}
as
(T)_{\bm{\alpha}}=T_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}
. In particular, when
T={\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}
, we have
(T)_{\bm{\alpha}}=v_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots v_{\alpha_{d}}
.

The inner product between two tensors
T,T^{\prime}\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
is given by

\langle T,T^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{\bm{\alpha}}T_{\bm{\alpha}}T^{\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}.

When
T={\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}
and
T^{\prime}={\bm{w}}^{\otimes k}
, it reduces to
\langle T,T^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{w}}\rangle^{k}
.

The Frobenius norm
\left\|T\right\|_{F}
and spectral norm
\left\|T\right\|_{2}
of a tensor
T\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
are respectively defined as

\left\|T\right\|_{F}\coloneqq\sqrt{\left\langle T,T\right\rangle},\quad\left\|T\right\|_{2}\coloneqq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bm{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{v}}_{k}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\\
\left\|{\bm{v}}_{1}\right\|=\cdots=\left\|{\bm{v}}_{k}\right\|=1\end{subarray}}|\left\langle T,{\bm{v}}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\bm{v}}_{k}\right\rangle|.

For symmetric tensors, it is known that the spectral norm also has the following equivalent definition (see
[
8
]
, Theorem 2.5):

Claim A.1
.

If
T\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
is symmetric, then

\left\|T\right\|_{2}=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bm{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{v}}_{k}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\\
\left\|{\bm{v}}_{1}\right\|=\cdots=\left\|{\bm{v}}_{k}\right\|=1\end{subarray}}|\left\langle T,{\bm{v}}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\bm{v}}_{k}\right\rangle|=\max_{{\bm{v}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d},\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|=1}|\left\langle T,{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle|.

We also have the following useful relation between Frobenius and spectral norms (see
[
30
]
).

Claim A.2
.

For any
T\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
, we have

\left\|T\right\|_{F}\leq d^{(k-1)/2}\left\|T\right\|_{2}.

Finally, for any matrix
W\in\mathbf{R}^{d\times d},{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the following identity holds for tensor inner products:

\left\langle(W^{\top}{\bm{u}})^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle=\left\langle W^{\top}{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\right\rangle^{k}=\left\langle{\bm{u}},W{\bm{v}}\right\rangle^{k}=\left\langle{\bm{u}}^{\otimes k},(W{\bm{v}})^{\otimes k}\right\rangle.

A.3
Hermite Tensor Polynomials

In this part, we give a brief introduction of Hermite polynomials.

Univariate Hermite Polynomials.

The probabilist’s Hermite polynomials in one dimension are defined as

\He_{k}(x)\coloneqq(-1)^{k}\exp\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}x^{k}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right).

Multivariate Hermite Tensors.

For a multivariate standard Gaussian, the degree-
k
Hermite tensor
\He_{k}({\bm{x}})\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
is defined as

(\He_{k}({\bm{x}}))_{\bm{\alpha}}\coloneqq\frac{(-1)^{k}}{\exp\left(\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|^{2}}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}}}\right)^{\bm{\alpha}}\exp\left(-\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|^{2}}{2}\right),

where
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\in[d]^{k}
and
\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}}}\right)^{\bm{\alpha}}=\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial x_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{\alpha_{k}}}
.

Properties.

A fundamental property of Hermite tensors
\{\He_{k}({\bm{x}})\}_{k}
is that they form an orthogonal basis with respect to the Gaussian measure. They also admit the following generating function:

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\left\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha\in[d]^{k}}\frac{\bm{t}^{\bm{\alpha}}}{k!}(\He_{|\bm{\alpha}|}({\bm{x}}))_{\bm{\alpha}}=\exp\left(\bm{t}^{\top}{\bm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{t}^{\top}\bm{t}\right),

(5)

where
\bm{t}^{\bm{\alpha}}=t_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots t_{\alpha_{k}}
and
\bm{\alpha}!=\alpha_{1}!\cdots\alpha_{k}!
.

This implies the following standard result:
See
2.2

The next lemma shows the orthogonality of Hermite tensors:

Claim A.3

(Orthogonality of Hermite tensors)
.

For
d
-dimensional Hermite tensors
\He_{j}({\bm{x}})

\He_{k}({\bm{x}})
and
{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
we have

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\left\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{s}}^{\otimes j}\right\rangle\left\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right]=\begin{cases}k!\left\langle{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\right\rangle^{k}&k=j\\
0&else\end{cases}.

We also have the following identity capturing how Hermite polynomials shift under translation:

Claim A.4

(Translation formula for Hermite polynomials)
.

\displaystyle\He_{n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}})=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}{\bm{u}}^{\otimes(n-j)}\otimes\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim N({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}})}\bigl[\langle\He_{n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes n}\rangle\,\langle\He_{\ell}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{w}}^{\otimes\ell}\rangle\bigr]=\mathbbm{1}_{\ell\leq n}\binom{n}{\ell}\ell!\langle{\bm{u}}^{\otimes(n-\ell)},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes(n-\ell)}\rangle\langle{\bm{v}}^{\otimes\ell},{\bm{w}}^{\otimes\ell}\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim N({\bm{\mu}},{\bm{I}})}\left[\left\langle\He_{k}({{\bm{x}}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle^{2}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{k}j!\binom{k}{j}^{2}\langle{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{v}}\rangle^{2k-2j}\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|^{2j}.

A.3.1
Omitted proofs

In this part, we give the omitted proofs for the lemma shown up in this section.
See
2.2

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}},{\bm{I}})}[\He_{k}({\bm{x}})]

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{\mu}}\right\|^{2}\right)\He_{k}({\bm{x}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}})}\left[\exp\left({\bm{\mu}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}{\bm{\mu}}^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}\right)\He_{k}({\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}})}\left[\left\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{\mu}}^{\otimes j}\right\rangle\He_{k}({\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle={\bm{\mu}}^{\otimes k}.

where the third identity follows from (
5
) and last identity is due to the orthogonality of Hermite tensors.
∎

See
A.3

Proof.

Recall the moment generating function of Gaussian is given by

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}[\exp\left(\bm{t}^{\top}{\bm{x}}\right)]=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{t}\right\|^{2}\right).

Then for
\forall{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, assume w.l.o.g. that
{\bm{s}}=(s_{1},s_{2},0,0,\ldots,0)^{\top},\bm{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},0,0,\ldots,0)^{\top}
, then

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}[\exp\left({\bm{s}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}+\bm{t}^{\top}{\bm{x}}\right)]=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}[\exp\left((s_{1}+t_{1})x_{1}+(s_{2}+t_{2})x_{2})\right)]\\
&=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+t_{1})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(s_{2}+t_{2})^{2}\right)=\exp\left(\frac{\left\|{\bm{s}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\bm{t}\right\|^{2}}{2}+\left\langle{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\right\rangle\right).\end{split}

So we get

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\exp\left({\bm{s}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\bm{s}}\right\|^{2}+\bm{t}^{\top}{\bm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{t}\right\|^{2}\right)\right]=\exp\left(\left\langle{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\right\rangle\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\left\langle{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\right\rangle^{i}.

On the other hand, note that
\exp\left({\bm{s}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\bm{s}}\right\|^{2}\right)
is the generating function for Hermite tensors, thus LHS can be expanded as

\begin{split}&\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\left\langle{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\right\rangle^{i}=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\exp\left({\bm{s}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\bm{s}}\right\|^{2}+\bm{t}^{\top}{\bm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{t}\right\|^{2}\right)\right]\\
&=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\left\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{s}}^{\otimes j}\right\rangle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\left\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right]\\
&=\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!k!}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\left\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{s}}^{\otimes j}\right\rangle\left\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right].\end{split}

Comparing coefficients of the
j,k
th order term above, we get

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\left\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{s}}^{\otimes j}\right\rangle\left\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right]=\begin{cases}k!\left\langle{\bm{s}},\bm{t}\right\rangle^{k}&k=j\\
0&else\end{cases}.

∎

See
A.4

Proof.

The proof is based on the tensor-valued generating function for probabilists’ Hermite polynomials (
5
).

item 1

Recall for
\bm{t}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the generating function in tensor form is

\exp\bigl(\langle\bm{t},{\bm{x}}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\rangle.

Also note that

\exp\bigl(\langle\bm{t},{\bm{u}}\rangle\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\langle{\bm{u}}^{\otimes k},\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\rangle.

This implies

\exp\bigl(\langle\bm{t},{\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)=\exp\bigl(\langle\bm{t},{\bm{u}}\rangle\bigr)\exp\bigl(\langle\bm{t},{\bm{x}}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\langle{\bm{u}}^{\otimes j},\bm{t}^{\otimes j}\rangle\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\rangle.

Also by generating function we know

\exp\bigl(\langle\bm{t},{\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}}),\bm{t}^{\otimes k}\rangle.

To read off the coefficient of
\bm{t}^{\otimes n}/n!
, set
k+j=n
. Writing
j=n-k
gives

\frac{1}{n!}\He_{n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{(n-k)!}{\bm{u}}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\frac{1}{k!}\He_{k}({\bm{x}}).

Thus,

\He_{n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}{\bm{u}}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\He_{k}({\bm{x}}).

item 2

From item 1 we can have

\langle\He_{n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes n}\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}\langle{\bm{u}}^{\otimes(n-j)},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes(n-j)}\rangle\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes j}\rangle.

Hence, due to orthogonality of Hermite polynomials, when
\ell>n
the below is 0 and when
\ell\leq n

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim N({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}})}\bigl[\langle\He_{n}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{u}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes n}\rangle\,\langle\He_{\ell}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{w}}^{\otimes\ell}\rangle\bigr]=

\displaystyle\binom{n}{\ell}\langle{\bm{u}}^{\otimes(n-\ell)},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes(n-\ell)}\rangle\mathbf{E}_{\bm{x}}[\langle\He_{\ell}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes l}\rangle\langle\He_{\ell}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{w}}^{\otimes\ell}\rangle]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{n}{\ell}\ell!\langle{\bm{u}}^{\otimes(n-\ell)},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes(n-\ell)}\rangle\langle{\bm{v}}^{\otimes\ell},{\bm{w}}^{\otimes\ell}\rangle.

item 3

Similar to item 2 we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim N({\bm{\mu}},{\bm{I}})}\left[\left\langle\He_{k}({{\bm{x}}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim N(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\left\langle\He_{k}({{\bm{x}}+{\bm{\mu}}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim N(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\langle{\bm{\mu}}^{\otimes(k-j)},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes(k-j)}\rangle\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes j}\rangle\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}^{2}\langle{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{v}}\rangle^{2k-2j}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim N(0,{\bm{I}})}\left[\langle\He_{j}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes j}\rangle^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k}j!\binom{k}{j}^{2}\langle{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{v}}\rangle^{2k-2j}\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|^{2j}.

where the fourth identity is based on Claim
A.3
.
∎

A.4
Comments on the algorithm

Here, we clarify what we mean by “optimality” in line 4 of Algorithm
1
and line 5 in Algorithm
2
.

Recall the optimization over
\bm{\pi}
is a constrained optimization problem over simplex
{\mathcal{P}}=\{\bm{\pi}:{\bm{1}}^{\top}\bm{\pi}=1,\bm{\pi}\geq 0\}
. We define the Lagrangian
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
with
{\bm{z}}\leq 0
and
\lambda
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\pi},\lambda,{\bm{z}})=\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},\lambda,{\bm{z}})+\lambda({\bm{1}}^{\top}\bm{\pi}-1)+{\bm{z}}^{\top}\bm{\pi}.

The first-order optimality condition becomes

0=\nabla_{\bm{\pi}}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}=\nabla_{\bm{\pi}}\mathcal{L}+\lambda{\bm{1}}+{\bm{z}},\quad{\bm{1}}^{\top}\bm{\pi}=1,\quad z_{i}\pi_{i}=0\ \forall i.

We consider an approximate version of these conditions, which we refer to as
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
-optimality:

\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\pi}}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\pi}}\mathcal{L}+\lambda{\bm{1}}+{\bm{z}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime}/2,\quad{\bm{1}}^{\top}\bm{\pi}=1,\quad|z_{i}\pi_{i}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime}/2\ \forall i.

Then, for any
i
we have

\pi_{i}|\nabla_{\pi_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi})+\lambda|\leq\pi_{i}|\nabla_{\pi_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi})+\lambda+z_{i}|+|z_{i}\pi_{i}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime}.

Recall
\nabla_{\pi_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi})=\mathbf{E}[\phi({\bm{\mu}}_{i};{\bm{x}})/p({\bm{x}})]
. Then,
|1+\lambda|=|\sum_{i}\pi_{i}\nabla_{\pi_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi})+\sum_{i}\pi_{i}\lambda|\leq\sum_{i}\pi_{i}|\nabla_{\pi_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi})+\lambda|\leq{\varepsilon}
, so
\lambda=1\pm{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
. Therefore, the
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
-optimality condition implies for all
i
:

\pi_{i}|\nabla_{\pi_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi})+1|\leq 2{\varepsilon}^{\prime}.

In the later analysis, we assume this condition holds with
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\poly({\varepsilon})
. This is what we mean by a
\poly({\varepsilon})
-optimal solution in Algorithm
1
.

Appendix B
Identifiability

The goal of this section is to show the following result of identifiability, which is the formal version of Theorem
4.11
.

Theorem B.1

(Identifiability)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, suppose there exists partition
[n]=\bigcup_{l\in[m]}S_{l}
such that
\forall i\in S_{l},\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}\right\|\leq B
. If
D_{\min}\geq 2\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}B
and
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})\leq{\varepsilon}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\pi_{\min}^{*^{8}}}{m^{4}n^{2}d^{2}}\lambda_{\min}^{6}D_{\max}^{-16}\right)
, then the following conditions are true:

•

(Weighted distance) For
\forall j\in[m]
,

\sum_{i\in S_{j}}\pi_{i}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{mnd}{\pi_{\min}^{*^{7/2}}}\lambda_{\min}^{-3}D_{\max}^{10}\right)\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}.

(6)

•

(Group weight) For
\forall j\in[m]
,

\left|\sum_{i\in S_{j}}\pi_{i}-\pi_{j}^{*}\right|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m^{2}n}{\pi_{\min}^{*^{3}}}\lambda_{\min}^{-2}D_{\max}^{6}\right)\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}.

(7)

•

(Close-by weight) For each
j\in[m]
, define
S_{j}(\delta_{\rm close})\coloneqq\{i\in[n]:i\in S_{j},\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|\leq\delta\}
where
\delta\coloneqq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{mnd}}{\pi_{\min}^{*^{9/4}}}\lambda_{\min}^{-3/2}D_{\max}^{5}\right){{\varepsilon}}^{1/4}
. Then

\sum_{i\in S_{j}(\delta_{\rm close})}\pi_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2}\pi_{j}^{*}.

(8)

•

(Average component) For
\forall j\in[m]
,

\left\|\sum_{i\in S_{j}}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}-\pi_{j}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|{\leq}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m^{2}nd}{\pi_{\min}^{*^{4}}}\lambda_{\min}^{-7/2}D_{\max}^{21/2}\right)\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}.

(9)

Below, we follow the 3-step outline in Section
5
to show it: step (a) handling KL-divergence in Appendix
B.1
, step (b) using test function to transform the problem into tensor decomposition problem in Appendix
B.2
, step (c) using whitening matrix techniques to obtain orthogonal tensor decomposition in Appendix
B.3
, and finally the proof of Theorem
B.1
in Appendix
B.4

B.1
Handling KL-divergence

In this part, we reduce KL divergence to chi-square like form as step (a) in Section
5
.

Definition 2

(
\chi^{2}
Divergence)
.

The chi-squared divergence between two distributions
p,q
is defined as:

\chi^{2}(p||q)\coloneqq\int_{{\bm{x}}}q({\bm{x}})\left(\frac{p({\bm{x}})}{q({\bm{x}})}-1\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}=\int_{{\bm{x}}}\frac{(p({\bm{x}})-q({\bm{x}}))^{2}}{q({\bm{x}})}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}.

We will utilize the following results from
[
32
]
that relates KL divergence to
\chi^{2}
divergence.

Theorem B.2

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
p,q
be two distributions and define

p_{\lambda}\coloneqq(1-\lambda)p+\lambda q

as the convex combination of
p
and
q
. Then for
\forall\lambda\in[0,1]
,

D_{KL}(p||p_{\lambda})=\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{1}{s}\chi^{2}(p||p_{s})\mathrm{d}s.

Proof.

Note that

\ln x=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{x-1}{x+(1-x)v}\mathrm{d}v,\forall x>0.

Therefore

\begin{split}D_{KL}(p||p_{\lambda})&=\int p({\bm{x}})\ln\left(\frac{p({\bm{x}})}{p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})}\right)\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\\
&=\int\int_{0}^{1}p({\bm{x}})\frac{p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})}{p({\bm{x}})+(p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}}))v}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\int p({\bm{x}})\frac{p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})}{p({\bm{x}})+(p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}}))v}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\mathrm{d}v.\end{split}

(10)

The inner integral above satisfies that

\displaystyle\int p({\bm{x}})\frac{p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})}{p({\bm{x}})+(p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}}))v}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\int(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}}))\left(1+\frac{(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}}))v}{p({\bm{x}})-(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}}))v}\right)\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\int(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda})({\bm{x}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+v\int\frac{(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}}))^{2}}{p({\bm{x}})-(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}}))v}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=v\int\frac{(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}}))^{2}}{p_{\lambda v}({\bm{x}})}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v}\int\frac{(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda v}({\bm{x}}))^{2}}{p_{\lambda v}({\bm{x}})}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v}\chi^{2}(p||p_{\lambda v}),

where the third equality is because
p({\bm{x}})-(p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}}))v=(1-\lambda v)p({\bm{x}})+\lambda vq({\bm{x}})=p_{\lambda v}({\bm{x}})
.

For
\lambda\in(0,1]
, plugging the above back into (
10
) yields that

\begin{split}D_{KL}(p||p_{\lambda})&=\int_{0}^{1}\int p({\bm{x}})\frac{p({\bm{x}})-p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})}{p({\bm{x}})+(p_{\lambda}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}}))v}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\mathrm{d}v\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{v}\chi^{2}(p||p_{\lambda v})\mathrm{d}v\\
&=\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{1}{s}\chi^{2}(p||p_{s})\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

The case for
\lambda=0
is trivial, so our proof is done.

∎

Base on the above theorem, we also have the following corollary:

Corollary B.3

(Proposition 1(c) in
[
32
]
)
.

Let
p,q
be two distributions. Then for
\forall\theta\in(0,1)
, the KL-divergence between
p
and
q
has lower bound

D_{\text{KL}}(p||q)\geq(1-\theta)\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\theta}\right)D_{\phi_{\theta}}(p||q)=\frac{1-\theta}{\theta^{2}}\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\theta}\right)\chi^{2}(p||(1-\theta)p+\theta q).

B.2
Local geometry bound using test function

In this part, we use Hermite polynomial as the test function to reduce the identifiability problem to a tensor decomposition problem. This is the step (b) in Section
5
.

We first argue Assumption
2
that
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|
are lower bounded is w.l.o.g.. We need such regularity condition in the later analysis.

Lemma B.4
.

If
\Delta\leq\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|\leq D,\forall i\neq j,i,j\in[m]
, then there exists
{\bm{v}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that for
\forall i\in[m]
we have

D\geq\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}-{\bm{v}}\right\|\geq\Delta/2.

(11)

Therefore, we can assume w.l.o.g. that
\forall i\in[m],\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|\in[\Delta/2,D]
by setting the origin point at
{\bm{v}}
.

Proof.

Assume w.l.o.g. that
{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}
and
{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}
are the two vectors in
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}\}_{l\in[m]}
with minimum pairwise distance, i.e.,
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|_{2}=\min_{k\neq l}\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{k}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}\right\|_{2}
. Now we claim that setting
{\bm{v}}=\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}
satisfies condition (
11
).

First we prove that
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-{\bm{v}}\right\|_{2}\geq\Delta/2,\forall l\in[m]
.

Assume for contradiction that
\exists l\in[m]
such that
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}\right\|_{2}<\Delta/2
. Consider two cases:

•

l\in\{i,j\}
:
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}\right\|_{2}\geq\Delta/2
, contradiction.

•

l\notin\{i,j\}
: Note that

\begin{split}&\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|_{2}<\Delta/2+\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}-\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}+{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|_{2}=\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|_{2}\end{split}

contradicts the minimality of
\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}\right\|_{2}
.

To prove the other half of (
11
), by triangle inequalities for
\forall l\in[m]
we have

\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-{\bm{v}}\right\|=\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}{2}+\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}\right\|\leq\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}{2}\right\|+\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}-{\bm{\mu}}_{j}^{*}}{2}\right\|\leq D/2+D/2=D.

∎

The result below uses test function to reduce minimizing KL divergence to a tensor decomposition problem. To see this, recall
g_{k}({\bm{x}})=\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\rangle
, then by Claim
2.2
we have

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p^{*}}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]=\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}\left\langle{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}\left\langle{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle=\left\langle\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*\otimes k}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle.

Lemma B.5

(Loss lower bound with test function)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, for any
k\in\{2,3,4\}
, let
g_{k}({\bm{x}})=\langle\He_{k}({\bm{x}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\rangle
, we have

\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})\geq\mathcal{O}\left(D_{\max}^{-2k}\right)\left(\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{*}}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]\right)^{2}.

Proof.

For
\forall\theta\in(0,1]
we have

\begin{split}&\quad\left|\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p_{*}}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}(p_{*}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}}))g_{k}({\bm{x}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\right|\\
&=\left|\mathbf{E}_{x\sim\theta p({\bm{x}})+(1-\theta)p_{*}({\bm{x}})}\left[\frac{p_{*}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}})}{\theta p({\bm{x}})+(1-\theta)p_{*}({\bm{x}})}g_{k}({\bm{x}})\right]\right|\\
&\leq\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{x\sim\theta p({\bm{x}})+(1-\theta)p_{*}({\bm{x}})}\left[\left(\frac{p_{*}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}})}{\theta p({\bm{x}})+(1-\theta)p_{*}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{x\sim\theta p({\bm{x}})+(1-\theta)p_{*}({\bm{x}})}\left[g_{k}^{2}({\bm{x}})\right]}\\
&=\frac{1}{\theta}\sqrt{\chi^{2}(p_{*}||\theta p+(1-\theta)p_{*})}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{x\sim\theta p+(1-\theta)p_{*}}\left[g_{k}^{2}({\bm{x}})\right]}\end{split}

(12)

Since
k\in\{2,3,4\}
is a constant and
\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|=1
, by Claim
A.4
we know that for
\forall{\bm{\mu}}
,

\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}},I)}\left[\left\langle\He_{k}({{\bm{x}}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle^{2}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{k}j!\binom{k}{j}^{2}\langle{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{v}}\rangle^{2k-2j}\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|^{2j}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|{\bm{\mu}}\right\|^{2k}\right).

So we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{E}_{x\sim\theta p+(1-\theta)p^{*}}\left[g_{k}^{2}({\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{x\sim\theta p+(1-\theta)p^{*}}\left[\left\langle\He_{k}({{\bm{x}}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\theta\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}}_{i},I)}\left[\left\langle\He_{k}({{\bm{x}}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\right)+(1-\theta)\left(\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*},I)}\left[\left\langle\He_{k}({{\bm{x}}}),{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}\right\|^{2k}\right)\right)+(1-\theta)\left(\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}^{*}\mathcal{O}\left(\left\|{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}\right\|^{2k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left((D_{\max}+B)^{2k}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(D_{\max}^{2k}\right).

So plugging the above back into (
12
) yields

\chi^{2}(p^{*}||\theta p+(1-\theta)p^{*})\geq\theta^{2}\mathcal{O}\left(D_{\max}^{-2k}\right)\left(\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p^{*}}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]\right)^{2}.

(13)

Theorem
B.2
and (
13
) jointly implies that (taking
\theta
as a constant)

\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})=D_{\text{KL}}(p^{*}||p)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\theta}\chi^{2}(p^{*}||\theta p+(1-\theta)p^{*})\mathrm{d}\theta\geq\mathcal{O}\left(D_{\max}^{-2k}\right)\left(\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p^{*}}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]\right)^{2}.

∎

B.3
Reduction to orthogonal tensor decomposition

In this part, we further reduce the problem to a orthogonal tensor decomposition problem, which leads to the final identifiability result. This the step (c) in Section
5
.

Denote the
k
-th order moment tensor as follows:

Definition 3
.

For any
k\in\mathbf{N}
, define
k
-th order moment tensor
M_{k}^{*}\in(\mathbf{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
as

{\bm{M}}_{k}^{*}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}^{\otimes k},\quad{\bm{M}}_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}^{\otimes k}

Particularly,
M_{2}^{*}=\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}^{\top}
.
Similarly, we define
M_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}^{\otimes k}
.

Recall in Section
5
we define whitened means
{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{*}\coloneqq\sqrt{\pi_{i}^{*}}{\bm{W}}^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}
and
{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{j}\coloneqq{\bm{W}}^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{j}
using whitening matrix
{\bm{W}}
, and show that
\{{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
are orthonormal. The following result translates the tensor decomposition problem into an orthogonal tensor decomposition problem in terms of
{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{*},{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}
.

Lemma B.6

(Identifiability as orthogonal tensor decomposition)
.

Define tensor decomposition error as

\mathcal{T}_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{l\in[m]}{\pi_{l}^{*}}^{1-k/2}{{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{l}}^{*^{\otimes k}}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{\otimes k}.

Under Assumption
1
,
2
,
3
, if the loss function has upper bound
\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})\leq{\varepsilon}
, then for
\forall k\in\{2,3,4\}
we have

\left\|\mathcal{T}_{k}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\sum_{l\in[m]}{\pi_{l}^{*}}^{1-k/2}{{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{l}}^{*^{\otimes k}}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{\otimes k}\right\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda_{\min}^{-k/2}D_{\max}^{k}\right)\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}.

(14)

Proof.

By Claim
2.2
and Lemma
B.5
, for
\forall{\bm{v}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
with
\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|=1
and
k\in\{2,3,4\}
we have

\mathcal{O}\left(D_{\max}^{2k}\right)\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})\geq(\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p^{*}}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})]-\mathbf{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim p}[g_{k}({\bm{x}})])^{2}=\left(\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}\left\langle{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}\left\langle{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right)^{2}.

Since both
M_{k}^{*}
and
M_{k}
are symmetric tensors, by Claim
A.1
we have the following bound:

\left\|M_{k}^{*}-M_{k}\right\|_{2}=\max_{\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|=1}\left|\left\langle M_{k}^{*}-M_{k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right|=\max_{\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|=1}\left|\left\langle\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}^{\otimes k}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(D_{\max}^{k}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})}.

Therefore, applying whitening matrix
{\bm{W}}
for
k\in\{2,3,4\}
we have

\begin{split}&\quad\left\|\sum_{l\in[m]}{\pi_{l}^{*}}^{1-k/2}{{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{l}}^{*^{\otimes k}}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}^{\otimes k}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\sum_{l\in[m]}\pi_{l}^{*}({W^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}})^{\otimes k}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}({W^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}})^{\otimes k}\right\|_{2}\\
&=\max_{\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|=1}\left|\left\langle\sum_{l\in[m]}\pi_{l}^{*}({W^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{l}^{*}})^{\otimes k}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}({W^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}_{i}})^{\otimes k},{\bm{v}}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\right|\\
&=\max_{\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|=1}\left\langle\sum_{i\in[m]}\pi_{i}^{*}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}^{*}}^{\otimes k}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\pi_{i}{{\bm{\mu}}_{i}}^{\otimes k},(W{\bm{v}})^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\\
&\leq\lambda_{\min}^{-k/2}\left\|M_{k}^{*}-M_{k}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq\lambda_{\min}^{-k/2}\mathcal{O}\left(D_{\max}^{k}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{L}(\bm{\pi},{\bm{\mu}})}\end{split}

where the second to last inequality is because
\left\|W\right\|_{2}=\lambda_{\min}^{-1/2}
.

∎

B.4
Proof of Theorem
B.1

We are now ready to show the main identifiability result for this section.
See
B.1

The key is to show the following conditions for
{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}
based on Lemma
B.6
from orthogonal tensor decomposition.

Lemma B.7
.

Under the setting of Theorem
B.1
, we have the following hold

•

For
\forall l\in[m],i\in[n],

\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda_{\min}^{-1/2}D_{\max}\right)\geq\left\|{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i}\right\|\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\max}^{-1/2}B.

(15)

•

For
\forall j,k\in[m],j\neq k,i\in S_{j}:

\left\langle{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{i},{\tilde{\bm{\mu}}}_{j}^{*}\right\rangle\geq 2\sqrt{\pi_{l}^{*}}\lambda_{\max}^{-1}B^{2}.

(16)
